Over time, the lack of a clear boundary between work and leisure led him to say yes to almost every opportunity, piling on late‑night planning sessions, last‑minute trips, and overlapping community projects, far beyond what his wife and friends felt he could reasonably juggle. For several months this escalating busyness seemed like an extension of his driven personality, until he abruptly shifted into a prolonged period of listlessness where he stopped attending arts council meetings, let his garden go, and lost interest in his photography outings along Buzzards Bay. When he missed a major exhibition opening he had organized and his wife found him sitting in the dark saying he couldn’t see the point of anything anymore, she persuaded him to discuss these swings with his primary care provider, who recognized a possible mood disorder and referred him to psychiatry.\n- Chief Complaint: Lately I just don’t care about the art shows, the mentoring, or even my photography anymore; nothing feels worth doing.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): For months now, even things that used to light me up—planning an exhibit, giving feedback to a young founder, or catching the light over Buzzards Bay with my camera—just feel like chores I can't be bothered to start, so I sit in my chair and let emails and invitations pile up unanswered.\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): These days I either lie awake until two or three in the morning staring at the ceiling, mind spinning or completely blank, and then wake up before dawn feeling wrung out, like I never really slept, even if I’ve technically been in bed for eight hours.\n- S028 (Psychomotor_Changes): My wife keeps pointing out how slowly I move now—I shuffle around the house, just stand in the kitchen staring at the kettle before making tea, and when someone asks me a question it takes me a long time to find the words and get them out, like I’m wading through mud.\n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): I keep telling myself that after all the advantages I’ve had I’ve turned into a useless old fraud, that retiring and then dropping the ball on the exhibition and the mentoring proves everyone would be better off if I just disappeared, and I replay every misstep from my career and marriage as if they’re unforgivable sins.\n- S032 (Death_Suicide): Most evenings I catch myself thinking it wouldn’t be so bad if I simply didn’t wake up tomorrow, and on my worse days I’ve stood on the bridge by the bay wondering what it would be like to just lean forward and be done, though I haven’t made any concrete plans to do that.\n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): Before this crash, there were weeks after I retired when I felt almost high on life—talking a mile a minute, saying yes to every project, getting snappish and downright furious with my wife if she so much as suggested I slow down or reschedule something.\n- S034 (Increased_Activity): Back in that busy stretch after I first left the firm, I was buzzing with energy, dashing from a breakfast meeting with a start‑up to an afternoon gallery visit to a late‑night Zoom call, barely sitting still long enough to eat and feeling irritated if anything forced me to stop.\n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): During that same period I honestly felt like I was the only person who could pull together the arts council, fix these fledgling companies, and write the definitive book on corporate strategy in retirement, as if I had some special insight no one else on the planet possessed.\n- S037 (Pressured_Speech): My wife jokes that a few months ago she couldn’t get a word in edgewise because I’d launch into long, rapid monologues about my memoir or the next show and just barrel on without pausing, even when she clearly looked tired or tried to change the subject.\n- S038 (Racing_Thoughts): When I was in that overdrive mode, my thoughts were racing so fast—from a new chapter idea to a marketing plan for a gallery to a phone call I should make—that it felt like a dozen radios were blaring in my head at once and I couldn’t slow any of them down enough to focus.\n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I went from a fairly measured retirement plan to cramming my calendar with overlapping mentoring sessions, committee meetings, and travel, constantly pacing the house between calls and brainstorming new ventures, mentoring commitments, missed the big exhibition opening I organized, and let my wife take over most of the household tasks because I can’t reliably show up or manage even simple responsibilities.\n- photography anymore; nothing feels worth doing.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): For months now, even things that used to light me up—planning an exhibit, giving feedback to a young founder, or catching the light over Buzzards Bay with my camera—just feel like chores I can't be bothered to start, so I sit in my chair and let emails and invitations pile up unanswered.\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): These days I either lie awake until two or three in the morning staring at the ceiling, mind spinning or completely blank, and then wake up before dawn feeling wrung out, like I never really slept, even if I’ve technically been in bed for eight hours.\n- S028 (Psychomotor_Changes): My wife keeps pointing out how slowly I move now—I shuffle around the house, just stand in the kitchen staring at the kettle before making tea, and when someone asks me a question it takes me a long time to find the words and get them out, like I’m wading through mud.\n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): I keep telling myself that after all the advantages I’ve had I’ve turned into a useless old fraud, that retiring and then dropping the ball on the exhibition and the mentoring proves everyone would be better off if I just disappeared, and I replay every misstep from my career and marriage as if they’re unforgivable sins.\n- S032 (Death_Suicide): Most evenings I catch myself thinking it wouldn’t be so bad if I simply didn’t wake up tomorrow, and on my worse days I’ve stood on the bridge by the bay wondering what it would be like to just lean forward and be done, though I haven’t made any concrete plans to do that.\n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): Before this crash, there were weeks after I retired when I felt almost high on life—talking a mile a minute, saying yes to every project, getting snappish and downright furious with my wife if she so much as suggested I slow down or reschedule something.\n- S034 (Increased_Activity): Back in that busy stretch after I first left the firm, I was buzzing with energy, dashing from a breakfast meeting with a start‑up to an afternoon gallery visit to a late‑night Zoom call, barely sitting still long enough to eat and feeling irritated if anything forced me to stop.\n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): During that same period I honestly felt like I was the only person who could pull together the arts council, fix these fledgling companies, and write the definitive book on corporate strategy in retirement, as if I had some special insight no one else on the planet possessed.\n- S037 (Pressured_Speech): My wife jokes that a few months ago she couldn’t get a word in edgewise because I’d launch into long, rapid monologues about my memoir or the next show and just barrel on without pausing, even when she clearly looked tired or tried to change the subject.\n- S038 (Racing_Thoughts): When I was in that overdrive mode, my thoughts were racing so fast—from a new chapter idea to a marketing plan for a gallery to a phone call I should make—that it felt like a dozen radios were blaring in my head at once and I couldn’t slow any of them down enough to focus.\n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I went from a fairly measured retirement plan to cramming my calendar with overlapping mentoring sessions, committee meetings, and travel, constantly pacing the house between calls and brainstorming new ventures, until it all collapsed and I couldn’t bring myself to follow through on any of it.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Since these swings started, I’ve had to back out of mentoring commitments, missed the big exhibition opening I organized, and let my wife take over most of the household tasks because I can’t reliably show up or manage even simple responsibilities.\n- duration: Looking back, these extreme highs and this long low stretch have been disrupting my life since a few months after I officially retired, so for about the past five months or so.\n-
